</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Saint Sava</b> (<a href="/wiki/Serbian_language" title="Serbian language">Serbian</a>: <span lang="sr">Свети Сава</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Serbian" title="Romanization of Serbian">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Serbian-language romanization"><i lang="sr-Latn">Sveti Sava</i></span>, <small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sh-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian" title="Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian">[sʋɛ̂ːtiː sǎːʋa]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic" title="Old Church Slavonic">Old Church Slavonic</a>: <span title="Church Slavonic-language text"><span lang="cu">Свѧтъ Сава / <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1113239643">.mw-parser-output .script-Cyrs{font-family:"Ponomar Unicode","Ponomar Unicode TT","Acathist","Triodion Unicode","Menaion Unicode","Menaion Unicode TT","Shafarik","Fedorovsk Unicode","Fedorovsk Unicode TT","Monomakh Unicode","Monomakh Unicode TT","Vilnius",BukyVede,"Kliment Std","RomanCyrillic Std","Monomachus","Old Standard","Old Standard TT",Dilyana,Menaion,"Menaion Medieval",Lazov,Code2000,"DejaVu Sans","DejaVu Serif",Code2001,"FreeSerif","TITUS Cyberbit Basic","Charis SIL","Doulos SIL","Chrysanthi Unicode","Bitstream Cyberbit","Bitstream CyberBase",Thryomanes,"Lucida Grande","FreeSans","Arial Unicode MS","Microsoft Sans Serif","Lucida Sans Unicode"}.mw-parser-output .script-Glag{font-family:Shafarik,"Menaion Unicode TT","Menaion Unicode",Vikidemia,Bukyvede,FreeSerif,Ja,Unicode5,"TITUS Cyberbit Basic","Noto Sans Glagolitic","Segoe UI Historic","Segoe UI Symbol"}</style><span class="script-Glag">ⰔⰂⰤⰕⰟ ⰔⰀⰂⰀ</span></span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Άγιος Σάββας</span>; 1169 or 1174 – 14 January 1236), known as <b>the Enlightener</b>, was a <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbian</a> prince and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a> monk, the first <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">Archbishop</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Autocephaly" title="Autocephaly">autocephalous</a> <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Church</a>, the founder of <a href="/wiki/Law_of_Serbia" title="Law of Serbia">Serbian law</a>, and a <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Serbia" title="Foreign relations of Serbia">diplomat</a>. Sava, born as <b>Rastko Nemanjić</b> (<a href="/wiki/Serbian_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Serbian Cyrillic alphabet">Serbian Cyrillic</a>: <span lang="sr-Cyrl">Растко Немањић</span>), was the youngest son of <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Grand_Prince" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian Grand Prince">Serbian Grand Prince</a> <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Nemanja" title="Stefan Nemanja">Stefan Nemanja</a> (founder of the <a href="/wiki/Nemanji%C4%87_dynasty" title="Nemanjić dynasty">Nemanjić dynasty</a>), and ruled the appanage of <a href="/wiki/Zachlumia" title="Zachlumia">Zachlumia</a> briefly in 1190–92. He then left for <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a>, where he became a monk with the name <i>Sava</i> (<i><a href="/wiki/Sabbas" title="Sabbas">Sabbas</a></i>). At Athos he established the monastery of <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a>, which became one of the most important cultural and religious centres of the Serbian people. In 1219 the <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople">Patriarchate</a> exiled in <a href="/wiki/Nicea" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicea">Nicea</a> recognized him as the first Serbian Archbishop, and in the same year he authored the oldest known constitution of Serbia, the <i><a href="/wiki/Zakonopravilo" title="Zakonopravilo">Zakonopravilo</a></i> <a href="/wiki/Nomocanon" title="Nomocanon">nomocanon</a>, thus securing full religious and political independence. Sava is regarded as the founder of <a href="/wiki/Serbian_medieval_literature" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian medieval literature">Serbian medieval literature</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDvornik196294–100_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDvornik196294–100-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMatejić1976_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatejić1976-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObolensky1988115–172_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObolensky1988115–172-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpeake201877–92_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake201877–92-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECurta201891–99_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta201891–99-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He is widely considered one of the most important figures of <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Serbs" title="History of the Serbs">Serbian history</a>. Sava is considered to be to the <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbs</a> what <a href="/wiki/Averroes" title="Averroes">Averroes</a> is to the <a href="/wiki/Muslims" title="Muslims">Muslims</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maimonides" title="Maimonides">Maimonides</a> is to the <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a>. Saint Sava is venerated by the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> on January 27&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> January 14&#93;. Many artistic works from the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> to modern times have interpreted his career. He is the patron saint of Serbia, Serbs, and Serbian education. The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Sava" title="Church of Saint Sava">Church of Saint Sava</a> in <a href="/wiki/Belgrade" title="Belgrade">Belgrade</a> is dedicated to him, built where the <a href="/wiki/Burning_of_Saint_Sava%27s_relics" title="Burning of Saint Sava&#39;s relics">Ottomans burnt his remains</a> in 1594,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERadić2007234_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERadić2007234-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> during an uprising in which Serbs used icons of Sava as their war flags; the church is one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_largest_church_buildings_in_the_world" class="mw-redirect" title="List of largest church buildings in the world">largest church buildings in the world</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Rastko (<a href="/wiki/Serbian_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Serbian Cyrillic alphabet">Serbian Cyrillic</a>: <span lang="sr-Cyrl">Растко Немањић</span>, <small>Serbian pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian" title="Help:IPA/Serbo-Croatian">[râstkɔ nɛ̌maɲitɕ]</a></span>), a diminutive of <i><a href="/wiki/Rastislav" class="mw-redirect" title="Rastislav">Rastislav</a></i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> was born in 1169 or 1174 <sup id="ref_a_2" class="reference"><a href="#cnote_a">[a]</a></sup>. As the youngest son of Grand Prince <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Nemanja" title="Stefan Nemanja">Stefan Nemanja</a> and his wife <a href="/wiki/Anastasia_of_Serbia" title="Anastasia of Serbia">Ana</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJakovljević2015423–432_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJakovljević2015423–432-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> prince Rastko belonged to the first generation of the <a href="/wiki/Nemanji%C4%87_dynasty" title="Nemanjić dynasty">Nemanjić dynasty</a>, alongside his brothers <a href="/wiki/Vukan_Nemanji%C4%87" title="Vukan Nemanjić">Vukan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Stefan_the_First-Crowned" title="Stefan the First-Crowned">Stefan</a>. His biographers mention that he was born after a hiatus in the couple's childbearing and was therefore especially dear. At the Serbian court the brothers received a good education<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine</a> tradition, which exercised great political, cultural and <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople">religious influence</a> in Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He grew up in a time of great foreign relations activities in Serbia. Rastko showed himself serious and <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a>; as the youngest son, he was made Prince of Hum at an early age,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> in ca. 1190.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine199419_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine199419-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Hum was a province between <a href="/wiki/Neretva_(river)" class="mw-redirect" title="Neretva (river)">Neretva</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dubrovnik" title="Dubrovnik">Dubrovnik</a> (<i>Ragusa</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Having his own court with magnates (<i>velmože</i>), senior officials and selected local <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Serbian_nobility" title="Medieval Serbian nobility">nobility</a>, the governance in Hum was not only an honorary title but constituted a practical school of state administration. <a href="/wiki/Teodosije_the_Hilandarian" title="Teodosije the Hilandarian">Teodosije the Hilandarian</a> said that Rastko, as a ruler, was "mild and gentle, kind to everyone, loving the poor as few others, and very respecting of the monastic life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He showed no interest in fame, wealth, or the throne.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The governance of Hum had previously been held by his uncle <a href="/wiki/Miroslav_of_Hum" title="Miroslav of Hum">Miroslav</a>, who continued to hold at least the <a href="/wiki/Lim_river" class="mw-redirect" title="Lim river">Lim region</a> with <a href="/wiki/Bijelo_Polje" title="Bijelo Polje">Bijelo Polje</a> while Rastko held Hum.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine199452_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine199452-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> After two years, in autumn 1192 or shortly afterwards, Rastko left Hum for <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Miroslav may have continued as ruler of Hum after Rastko had left.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine199420_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine199420-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Athonite monks were frequent visitors to the Serbian court – lectures perhaps made him determined to leave.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mount_Athos">Mount Athos</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Mount Athos">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796,_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png/220px-St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="361" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png/330px-St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png/440px-St._Sava_and_Nemanja_1796%2C_Bay_of_Kotor_painting_school.png 2x" data-file-width="1900" data-file-height="3119" /></a><figcaption>Late 18th century <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a> of Saint Sava and his father Saint Simeon</figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon arriving at Athos, Rastko entered the Russian <a href="/wiki/St._Panteleimon_Monastery" title="St. Panteleimon Monastery">St. Panteleimon Monastery</a> where he received the monastic name of <i>Sava</i> (<a href="/wiki/Sabbas" title="Sabbas">Sabbas</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> According to tradition, a Russian monk was his spiritual guide or mentor<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and was said to have had earlier visited the Serbian court with other Athonite monks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Sava then entered the Greek <a href="/wiki/Vatopedi" title="Vatopedi">Vatopedi</a> monastery, where he would stay for the next seven years, and became more closely acquainted with Greek theological and church-administrational literature. His father tried to persuade him to return to Serbia, but Sava was determined and replied, "You have accomplished all that a Christian sovereign should do; come now and join me in the true Christian life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> His young years at Athos had a significant influence on the formation of his personality, it was also here that he found models on which he would organize monastic and church life in Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObolensky1974291_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObolensky1974291-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObolensky1988115_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObolensky1988115-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Stefan Nemanja took his son's advice<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> – he summoned an assembly at <a href="/wiki/Studenica_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Studenica monastery">Studenica</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abdicated" class="mw-redirect" title="Abdicated">abdicated</a> on 25 March 1196, giving the throne to his middle son, <a href="/wiki/Stefan_the_First-Crowned" title="Stefan the First-Crowned">Stefan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The next day, Nemanja and his wife Ana took monastic vows.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038_12-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200038-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Nemanja took the monastic name <i>Simeon</i> and stayed in Studenica until leaving for Mount Athos in autumn 1197.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200030_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200030-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The arrival was greatly pleasing to Sava and the Athonite community, as Nemanja as a ruler had donated much to the community. The two, with consent of <i><a href="/wiki/Hegumen" title="Hegumen">hegumen</a></i> (monastery head) Theostyriktos of Vatopedi, went on a tour of Athos in late autumn 1197 in order for Simeon to familiarize with all of its churches and sacred places; Nemanja and Ana donated to numerous monasteries, especially Karyes, Iviron and the Great Lavra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Sava visited the Byzantine Emperor <a href="/wiki/Alexios_III_Angelos" title="Alexios III Angelos">Alexios III Angelos</a> at Constantinople, he mentioned the neglected and abandoned <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a>, and asked the Emperor that he and his father be given the permit to restore the monastery and grant it to Vatopedi.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor approved, and sent a special letter and considerable gold to his friend Stefan Nemanja (<a href="/wiki/Monk_Simeon" title="Monk Simeon">monk Simeon</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Sava then addressed the <a href="/wiki/Protos_(monastic_office)" title="Protos (monastic office)">Protos</a> of Athos, asking them to support the effort so the monastery of Hilandar might become the haven for Serb monks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> All Athonite monasteries, except Vatopedi, accepted the proposal. In July 1198 Emperor Alexios III authored a charter which revoked the earlier decision, and instead not only granted Hilandar, but also the other abandoned monasteries in Mileis, to Simeon and Sava, to be a haven and shelter for Serb monks in Athos.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The restoration of Hilandar quickly began and Grand Prince Stefan sent money and other necessities, and issued the founding charter for Hilandar in 1199.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sava wrote a <i><a href="/wiki/Typikon" title="Typikon">typikon</a></i> (liturgical office order) for Hilandar, modeled on the <i>typikon</i> of the monastery of <a href="/wiki/Theotokos_Euergetis" class="mw-redirect" title="Theotokos Euergetis">Theotokos Euergetis</a> in Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Besides Hilandar, Sava was the <i><a href="/wiki/Ktetor" title="Ktetor">ktetor</a></i> (sr. <i>ktitor</i>; founder, donator) of the hermitage at <a href="/wiki/Karyes_(Athos)" class="mw-redirect" title="Karyes (Athos)">Karyes</a> (seat of Athos) for the monks who devoted themselves to solitude and prayer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In 1199, he authored the <a href="/wiki/Karyes_Typikon" title="Karyes Typikon">typikon of Karyes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Along with the hermitage, he built the chapel dedicated to <a href="/wiki/Sabbas_the_Sanctified" title="Sabbas the Sanctified">Sabbas the Sanctified</a>, whose name he received upon monastic vows.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> His father died on 13 February 1199.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200030_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200030-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In 1204, after 13 April, Sava received the rank of <i><a href="/wiki/Archimandrite" title="Archimandrite">archimandrite</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpeake201883_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake201883-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Nemanja had earlier (1196) decided to give the rule to <a href="/wiki/Stefan_the_First-Crowned" title="Stefan the First-Crowned">Stefan</a>, and not the eldest son, <a href="/wiki/Vukan_Nemanji%C4%87" title="Vukan Nemanjić">Vukan</a>, the latter began plotting against Stefan in the meantime. He found an ally in Hungarian king <a href="/wiki/Emeric,_King_of_Hungary" title="Emeric, King of Hungary">Emeric</a> with whom he banished Stefan to Bulgaria, and Vukan seized the Serbian throne (1202). Stefan returned to Serbia with an army in 1204 and pushed Vukan to <a href="/wiki/Zeta_(crown_land)" title="Zeta (crown land)">Zeta</a>, his hereditary land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine199441–48_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine199441–48-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> After problems at Athos with Latin bishops and <a href="/wiki/Boniface_of_Montferrat" class="mw-redirect" title="Boniface of Montferrat">Boniface of Montferrat</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Crusade" title="Fourth Crusade">Fourth Crusade</a>, Sava returned to Serbia in the winter of 1205–1206 or 1206–1207, with the remains of his father which he relocated to his father's endowment, the <a href="/wiki/Studenica_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Studenica monastery">Studenica monastery</a>, and then reconciled his quarreling brothers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine199479_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine199479-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Sava saved the country from further political crisis by ending the dynastic fight, and also completed the canonization process of Nemanja (Simeon) as a saint.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆirkovićKoraćBabić198613_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆirkovićKoraćBabić198613-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBataković200519_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBataković200519-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Enlightenment">Enlightenment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Enlightenment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad,_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87,_1921.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg/220px-Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="306" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg/330px-Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg/440px-Sveti_Sava_blagosilja_Srp%C4%8Dad%2C_Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87%2C_1921.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1654" data-file-height="2302" /></a><figcaption>Sava blessing Serb youth, <a href="/wiki/Uro%C5%A1_Predi%C4%87" title="Uroš Predić">Uroš Predić</a> (1921).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Having spent 14 years in Mount Athos, Sava had extensive theological knowledge and spiritual power.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039_19-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200039-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> According to Sava's biography, he was asked to teach the court and people of Serbia the Christian laws and traditions and "in that way enwisen and educate".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Sava then worked on the religious and cultural enlightenment of the Serbian people, educating in Christian morality, love and mercy, meanwhile also working on the church organization.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Since his return in 1206, he became the <i><a href="/wiki/Hegumen" title="Hegumen">hegumen</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Studenica_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Studenica monastery">Studenica</a>, and as its elder, self-willed entered regulations on the independent status of that monastery in the Studenica Typikon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpeake201883_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake201883-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He used the general chaos in which the Byzantine Empire found itself after the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_(1204)" class="mw-redirect" title="Siege of Constantinople (1204)">siege of Constantinople (1204)</a> into the hands of the Crusaders, and the strained relations between the <a href="/wiki/Despotate_of_Epirus" title="Despotate of Epirus">Despotate of Epirus</a> (where the <a href="/wiki/Archbishopric_of_Ohrid" title="Archbishopric of Ohrid">Archbishopric of Ohrid</a> was seated, which the Serbian Church was subordinated to) and the <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople">Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nicaea" title="Nicaea">Nicaea</a>, into his advantage. The Studenica Typikon became a sort of <i><a href="/wiki/Lex_specialis" title="Lex specialis">lex specialis</a></i>, which allowed Studenica to have independent status ("Here, therefore, no one is to have authority, neither bishop nor any one else") in relation to the Bishopric of Raška and Archbishopric of Ohrid. The canonization of Nemanja and the Studenica Typikon would be the first steps towards the future autocephaly of the Serbian Church and elevation of the Serbian ruler to king ten years later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆirkovićKoraćBabić198616–19_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆirkovićKoraćBabić198616–19-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpeake201883_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake201883-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3,_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg/220px-%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg/330px-%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg/440px-%D0%A1%D0%B2%D0%B5%D1%82%D0%B8_%D0%A1%D0%B0%D0%B2%D0%B0_%D0%BA%D1%80%D1%83%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%88%D0%B5_%D1%81%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B3_%D0%B1%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A1%D1%82%D0%B5%D1%84%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B0_%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%87%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B3%2C_by_Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="884" /></a><figcaption>Crowning of Stefan, by <a href="/wiki/Anastas_Jovanovi%C4%87" title="Anastas Jovanović">Anastas Jovanović</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1217, archimandrite Sava left Studenica and returned to Mount Athos. His departure has been interpreted by a part of the historians as a reaction to his brother Stefan accepting the royal crown from Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Stefan had just prior to this made a large switch in politics, marrying a Venetian noblewoman, and subsequently asked the Pope for a royal crown and political support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆirković200438_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆirković200438-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> With the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Latin_Empire" title="Latin Empire">Latin Empire</a> (1204), Rome had considerably increased its power in the Balkans. Stefan was crowned by a papal legate, becoming equal to the other kings, and was called "the First-Crowned King" of Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994107_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994107-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKalić20178–9,_15_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalić20178–9,_15-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Stefan's politics that led to the events of 1217 were somewhat in odds with the Serbian Orthodox tradition, represented by his brother, archimandrite Sava, who favored Eastern Orthodoxy and Byzantine ecclesiastical culture in Serbia. Though Sava left Serbia while talks were underway between Stefan and Rome (apparently due to disagreeing with Stefan's excessive reliance on Rome), he and his brother resumed their good relation after receiving the crown. It is possible that Sava did not agree with everything in his brother's international politics, however, his departure for Athos may also be interpreted as a preparation for obtaining the autocephaly (independence) of the Serbian Archbishopric.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> His departure was planned, both Domentijan and Teodosije, Sava's biographers, stated that before leaving Studenica he appointed a new <i>hegumen</i> and "put the monastery in good, correct order, and enacted the new church constitution and monastic life order, to be held that way", after which he left Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sveti_Sava,_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg/200px-Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="556" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg/300px-Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg/400px-Sveti_Sava%2C_Mile%C5%A1eva.jpg 2x" data-file-width="527" data-file-height="1464" /></a><figcaption>Fresco in <a href="/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_Monastery" title="Mileševa Monastery">Mileševa</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Autocephaly_and_church_organization">Autocephaly and church organization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Autocephaly and church organization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1e/Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg/220px-Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="301" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1e/Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg/330px-Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1e/Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg/440px-Sveti_Sava_miri_zava%C4%91enu_bra%C4%87u.jpg 2x" data-file-width="450" data-file-height="616" /></a><figcaption>Sava reconciling his quarreling brothers, <a href="/wiki/Paja_Jovanovi%C4%87" title="Paja Jovanović">Paja Jovanović</a> (1901)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The elevation of Serbia into a kingdom did not fully mark the independence of the country, according to that time's understanding, unless the same was achieved with its church. Rulers of such countries, with church bodies subordinated to Constantinople, were viewed as "rulers of lower status who stand under the top chief of the Orthodox Christian world – the Byzantine Emperor". Conditions in Serbia for <a href="/wiki/Autocephaly" title="Autocephaly">autocephaly</a> were largely met at the time, with a notable number of learned monks, regulated monastic life, stable church hierarchy, thus "its autocephaly, in a way, was only a question of time". It was important to Sava that the head of the Serbian church was appointed by Constantinople, and not Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994116–119_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994116–119-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 15 August 1219, during the feast of the <a href="/wiki/Dormition_of_the_Mother_of_God" title="Dormition of the Mother of God">Dormition of the Mother of God</a>, Sava was consecrated by <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_Manuel_I_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch Manuel I of Constantinople">Patriarch Manuel I of Constantinople</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nicaea" title="Nicaea">Nicaea</a> as the first Archbishop of the autocephalous (independent) Serbian Church. The patriarch of Constantinople and his Synod thus appointed Sava as the first archbishop of <i>"Serbian and coastal lands."</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstrogorsky1956383_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstrogorsky1956383-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlagojević199327–28_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlagojević199327–28-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆirković200428,_42–43_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆirković200428,_42–43-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerjančićMaksimović201437–54_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerjančićMaksimović201437–54-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarjanović201841–50_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarjanović201841–50-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> With the support of Emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodore_I_Laskaris" title="Theodore I Laskaris">Theodore I Laskaris</a> and "the Most Venerable Patriarch and the whole Constantinopolitan assembly" he received the blessing that Serbian archbishops receive consecration from their own bishops' assemblies without consenting with the <a href="/wiki/Latin_Patriarch_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin Patriarch of Constantinople">Latin Patriarch of Constantinople</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Sava had thus secured the independence of the church; in the Middle Ages, the church was the supporter and important factor in state sovereignty, and political and national identity. At the same time, both Laskaris and Manuel were delighted that Serbian policy was continuously looking towards <a href="/wiki/Constantine_the_Great" title="Constantine the Great">Constantine the Great</a>'s legacy –  <a href="/wiki/Byzantium" title="Byzantium">Byzantium</a> – rather than Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Tricht2011402–405_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Tricht2011402–405-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From Nicaea, Archbishop Sava returned to Mount Athos, where he profusely donated to the monasteries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> In Hilandar, he addressed the question of administration: "he taught the hegumen especially how to, in every virtue, show himself as an example to others; and the brothers, once again, he taught how to listen to everything the hegumen said with the fear of God", as witnessed by Teodosije.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> From Hilandar, Sava traveled to <a href="/wiki/Thessaloniki" title="Thessaloniki">Thessaloniki</a>, to the monastery of Philokalos, where he stayed for some time as a guest of the Metropolitan of Thessaloniki, Constantine the Mesopotamian, with whom he was a great friend ever since his youth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040_25-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200040-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> His stay was of great benefit as he transcribed many works on law needed for his church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon his return to Serbia, he was engaged in the organization of the Serbian church, especially regarding the structure of bishoprics, those that were situated on locales at the sensitive border with the Roman Catholic West.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> At the assembly in <a href="/wiki/%C5%BDi%C4%8Da" title="Žiča">Žiča</a> in 1219, Sava "chose, from his pupils, God-understanding and God-fearing and honorable men, who were able in managing by divine laws and by the tradition of the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Apostles">Holy Apostles</a>, and keep the apparitions of the holy God-bearing fathers. And he consecrated them and made them bishops" (Domentijan).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Sava gave the newly appointed bishops law books and sent them to bishoprics in all parts of Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> It is unclear how many bishoprics he founded. The following bishoprics were under his administration: Zeta (<i>Zetska</i>), seated at Monastery of Holy Archangel Michael in <a href="/wiki/Prevlaka" title="Prevlaka">Prevlaka</a> near Kotor; Hum (<i>Humska</i>), seated at <a href="/wiki/Monastery_of_the_Holy_Mother_of_God,_Ston" title="Monastery of the Holy Mother of God, Ston">Monastery of the Holy Mother of God</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ston" title="Ston">Ston</a>; <a href="/wiki/Eparchy_of_Dabar" class="mw-redirect" title="Eparchy of Dabar">Dabar</a>, seated at Monastery of St. Nicholas on the Lim; Moravica, seated at Monastery of St. Achillius in the Moravica region; Budimlja, seated at Monastery of St. George; Toplica, seated at Monastery of St. Nicholas in the Toplica region; Hvosno, seated at Monastery of the Holy Mother of God in the <a href="/wiki/Hvosno" title="Hvosno">Hvosno</a> region; Žiča, seated at <a href="/wiki/%C5%BDi%C4%8Da" title="Žiča">Žiča</a>, the seat of the Church; Raška, seated at <a href="/wiki/Patriarchal_Monastery_of_Pe%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarchal Monastery of Peć">Monastery of Holy Apostles Peter and Paul</a> in <a href="/wiki/Pe%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Peć">Peć</a>; Lipljan, seated at <a href="/wiki/Lipljan" class="mw-redirect" title="Lipljan">Lipljan</a>; Prizren, seated at <a href="/wiki/Prizren" title="Prizren">Prizren</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPopović2002171–184_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopović2002171–184-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Among his bishops were <a href="/wiki/Ilarion_(medieval_Serbian_bishop)" title="Ilarion (medieval Serbian bishop)">Ilarion</a> and <a href="/wiki/Metodije_(medieval_Serbian_bishop)" title="Metodije (medieval Serbian bishop)">Metodije</a>. In the same year Sava published <i><a href="/wiki/Zakonopravilo" title="Zakonopravilo">Zakonopravilo</a></i> (or "St. Sava's Nomocanon"), the first <a href="/wiki/Constitution" title="Constitution">constitution</a> of Serbia; thus the Serbs acquired both forms of independence: political and religious.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994118_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994118-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDragojlović199336_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDragojlović199336-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg/220px-Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="295" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg/330px-Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg/440px-Sveti_Sava_Kraljeva_Crkva.jpg 2x" data-file-width="867" data-file-height="1161" /></a><figcaption>Fresco detail of Saint Sava in <a href="/wiki/Studenica_Monastery" title="Studenica Monastery">Studenica Monastery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The organizational work of Sava was very energetic, and above all, the new organization was given a clear national character. The Greek bishop at Prizren was replaced by a Serbian, his disciple. This was not the only feature of his fighting spirit. The determination of the seats of the newly established bishoprics was also performed with especially state-religious intention. The Archbishopric was seated in the <a href="/wiki/Monastery_of_%C5%BDi%C4%8Da" class="mw-redirect" title="Monastery of Žiča">Monastery of Žiča</a>, the new endowment of King Stefan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECurta2006393_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006393-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The bishopric in Dabar on the Lim river was situated towards the border with Bosnia, to act on the Orthodox element there and suppress the <a href="/wiki/Bosnian_Church" title="Bosnian Church">Bogomil teaching</a>. The bishopric of Zeta was located on the <a href="/wiki/Prevlaka" title="Prevlaka">Prevlaka</a> peninsula, <a href="/wiki/Bay_of_Kotor" title="Bay of Kotor">Bay of Kotor</a>, out of real <a href="/wiki/Zeta_(crown_land)" title="Zeta (crown land)">Zeta</a> itself, and the bishopric of Hum in <a href="/wiki/Ston" title="Ston">Ston</a>; both of these were almost on the outskirts of the kingdom, obviously with the aim to combat the Catholic action which had spread especially from the Catholic dioceses of Kotor and Dubrovnik. In earlier times, also Orthodox monasteries were subjected to the supervision of the Catholic Archdiocese of Bar; after Sava's action that intercourse began to change in the opposite direction. After Sava's organization, Orthodoxy finally became the state religion of Serbia. Sava, in that respect, worked consistently and without any regard. The Bogomils had been prohibited already by his father, Nemanja, while Sava, as an Athonite Latinophobe, did his part all to prevent and weaken the influence of Catholicism. Through his clergy, which he directly influenced as an example and with teaching, Sava rose also the general cultural level of the whole people, striving to develop human virtues and a sense of civic duty. The Serbian state thought of the Nemanjić dynasty was created politically by Nemanja, but spiritually and intellectually by Sava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarter196939–56_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarter196939–56-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDragojlović199332–40_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDragojlović199332–40-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_pilgrimage">First pilgrimage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: First pilgrimage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Marsava.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Marsava.jpg/220px-Marsava.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Marsava.jpg/330px-Marsava.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/9a/Marsava.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba" title="Mar Saba">Mar Saba</a>, where Sava founded Serbian cells</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:VergineTricherusa.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/VergineTricherusa.jpg/220px-VergineTricherusa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="278" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/95/VergineTricherusa.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="379" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Trojeru%C4%8Dica" title="Trojeručica">Trojeručica</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">serbian orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>After the crowning of his nephew <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Radoslav" title="Stefan Radoslav">Radoslav</a>, the son of Stefan, Sava left the Serbian maritime in 1229 for a trip to <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a>. He visited almost all the <a href="/wiki/List_of_religious_sites#Christianity" title="List of religious sites">holy places</a> and endowed them with valued gifts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The Patriarch of Jerusalem, <a href="/wiki/Athanasius_II_of_Jerusalem" title="Athanasius II of Jerusalem">Athanasius</a>, along with the rest of the prelates, and especially monks, warmly greeted and welcomed him. Sava asked Athanasios II, his host, and the Great Lavra fraternity, led by <i><a href="/wiki/Hegoumenos" class="mw-redirect" title="Hegoumenos">hegoumenos</a></i> Nicholas, if he could purchase two monasteries in the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a>. His request was accepted and he was offered the monasteries of Saint John the Theologian on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Zion" title="Mount Zion">Mount Sion</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=St._George%27s_Monastery,_Acre&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="St. George&#39;s Monastery, Acre (page does not exist)">St. George's Monastery</a> at <a href="/wiki/Acre,_Israel" title="Acre, Israel">Akkon</a> - both to be inhabited by Serbian monks. On the way back he visited <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Nicaea" title="Empire of Nicaea">Nicaea</a> and the Byzantine Emperor <a href="/wiki/John_Vatatzes" class="mw-redirect" title="John Vatatzes">John Vatatzes</a> (r. 1221–1254), where he remained for several days. From there, he continued his journey to Mount Athos, Hilandar, and then via <a href="/wiki/Thessaloniki" title="Thessaloniki">Thessaloniki</a> to Serbia. While visiting <a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba" title="Mar Saba">Mar Saba</a>, he had been gifted the <i><a href="/wiki/Trojeru%C4%8Dica" title="Trojeručica">Trojeručica</a></i> (the "Three-handed Theotokos"), an icon of <a href="/wiki/Nursing_Madonna" title="Nursing Madonna">Nursing Madonna</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Crosier" title="Crosier">crosier</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sabbas_the_Sanctified" title="Sabbas the Sanctified">Sabbas the Sanctified</a>, which he brought to Hilandar. After a short stay at Studenica, Sava embarked on a four-year trip throughout the lands where he confirmed the theological teachings and delivered constitutions and customs of monastic life to be kept, as he had seen in Mount Athos, Palestine, and the Middle East.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarković2009267–271_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarković2009267–271-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_pilgrimage_and_death">Second pilgrimage and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Second pilgrimage and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the throne change in 1234, when King Radoslav was succeeded by his brother <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Vladislav" title="Stefan Vladislav">Vladislav</a>, Archbishop Sava began his second trip to the Holy Land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Prior to this, Sava had appointed his loyal pupil <a href="/wiki/Arsenije_Sremac" title="Arsenije Sremac">Arsenije Sremac</a> as his successor to the throne of the Serbian Archbishopric.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Domentijan says that Sava chose Arsenije through his "clairvoyance", with Teodosije stating further that he was chosen because Sava knew he was "evil-less and more just than others, prequalified in all, always fearing God and carefully keeps His commandments".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> This move was wise and deliberate; still in his lifetime he chose himself a worthy successor because he knew that the further fate of the Serbian Church largely depended on the personality of the successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sava began his trip from <a href="/wiki/Budva" title="Budva">Budva</a>, then via <a href="/wiki/Brindisi" title="Brindisi">Brindisi</a> in Italy to <a href="/wiki/Acre,_Israel" title="Acre, Israel">Acre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> On this road he experienced various bad events, such as an organized pirate attack in the rough <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea" title="Mediterranean Sea">Mediterranean Sea</a>, which however ended well.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In Acre he stayed in his monastery dedicated to St. George, which he had earlier bought from the <a href="/wiki/Latin_Empire" title="Latin Empire">Latins</a>, and then from there went to <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, to the Monastery of St. John the Apostle, "which he, as soon as arriving, redeemed from the <a href="/wiki/Saracens" class="mw-redirect" title="Saracens">Saracens</a>, in his name".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Sava had a prolonged stay in Jerusalem; he was again friendly and brotherly received by Patriarch Athanasius.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> From Jerusalem he went to <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>, where he visited Patriarch Nicholas, with whom he exchanged gifts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
After touring the holy places in <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, he returned to Jerusalem, from where he went to the <a href="/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula" title="Sinai Peninsula">Sinai</a>, where he spent <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lent</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He returned briefly to Jerusalem, then went to <a href="/wiki/Principality_of_Antioch" title="Principality of Antioch">Antiochia</a>, and from there across <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a> and the "Turkic lands" he went on the "Syrian Sea" and then returned on a ship to Antiochia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> On the ship, Sava became sick, and was unable to eat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> After a longer trip he arrived at Constantinople where he briefly stayed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043_46-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Sava first wanted to return home via Mount Athos (according to Domentijan), but he instead decided to visit the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bulgarian_Empire" title="Second Bulgarian Empire">Bulgarian</a> capital at <a href="/wiki/Veliko_Tarnovo" title="Veliko Tarnovo">Tarnovo</a>, where he was warmly and friendly admitted by the Bulgarian Emperor <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Asen_II_of_Bulgaria" class="mw-redirect" title="Ivan Asen II of Bulgaria">Ivan Asen II</a> (father-in-law of King Vladislav) and Bulgarian Patriarch Joakim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDujčev197817–29_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDujčev197817–29-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_(Veliko_Tarnovo)_E3.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg/220px-SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg/330px-SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg/440px-SS.Forty_Martyrs_Church_%28Veliko_Tarnovo%29_E3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption>Sava died ill on his way home from the Holy Land, on 12 January 1235, in <a href="/wiki/Veliko_Tarnovo" title="Veliko Tarnovo">Tarnovo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Second_Bulgarian_Empire" title="Second Bulgarian Empire">Bulgarian Empire</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As on all his destinations, he gave rich gifts to the churches and monasteries: "[he] gave also to the Bulgarian Patriarchate priestly honourable robes and golden books and candlesticks adorned with precious stones and pearls and other church vessels", as written by Teodosije.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043–44_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200043–44-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Sava had after much work and many long trips arrived at Tarnovo a tired and sick man.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> When the sickness took a hold of him and he saw that the end was near, he sent part of his entourage to Serbia with the gifts and everything he had bought with his blessing to give "to his children".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Eulogia" title="Eulogia">eulogia</a> consisted of four items.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Domentijan accounted that he died between Saturday and Sunday, most likely on 27 January &#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 14 January&#93; 1235.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sava was respectfully buried at the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Forty_Martyrs_Church,_Veliko_Tarnovo" title="Holy Forty Martyrs Church, Veliko Tarnovo">Holy Forty Martyrs Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994136_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994136-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Sava's body was returned to Serbia after a series of requests,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994136_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994136-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> and was then buried in the <a href="/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Mileševa monastery">Mileševa monastery</a>, built by Vladislav in 1234.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994136_52-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994136-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECurta2019515_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2019515-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> According to Teodosije, Archbishop Arsenije told Vladislav "It's neither nice nor pleasing, before God nor the people, leaving our father [Sava] gifted to us by the Christ. An equal to apostles – who made so many feats and countless efforts for the Serbian lands, decorating it with churches and the kingdom, the archbishopric and bishops, and all constitutions and laws – that his relics lie outside his fatherland and the seat of his church, in a foreign land".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> King Vladislav twice sent delegations to his father-in-law Asen, asking him to let the relics of Sava be transferred to the fatherland, but the Emperor was unappealing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Vladislav then personally visited him and finally got the approval, and brought the relics to Serbia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> With the highest church- and state honours, the relics of Saint Sava were transferred from the Holy Forty Martyrs Church to <a href="/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Mileševa monastery">Mileševa</a> on 19 May &#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 6 May&#93; 1237. "The King and the Archbishop, with the bishops and hegumens and many noblemen, all together, little and great, carried the Saint in much joy, with psalms and songs".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Sava was canonized, and his relics were considered miraculous; his cult remained throughout the <a href="/wiki/Serbia_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="Serbia in the Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Serbia" title="Ottoman Serbia">Ottoman rule</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994136_52-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994136-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Sava,_Bogorodica_Ljevi%C5%A1ka.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b2/Saint_Sava%2C_Bogorodica_Ljevi%C5%A1ka.jpg" decoding="async" width="182" height="221" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="182" data-file-height="221" /></a><figcaption>Fresco from Saint Sava in <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Ljevi%C5%A1" title="Our Lady of Ljeviš">Monastery Bogorodica Ljeviška</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Saint Sava is the protector of the Serb people: he is venerated as a protector of churches, families, schools and artisans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> His feast day is also venerated by Greeks, Bulgarians, Romanians and Russians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>  Numerous toponyms and other testimonies, preserved to this day, convincingly speak of the prevalence of the cult of St. Sava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> St. Sava is regarded the father of Serbian education and literature; he authored the <i>Life of St. Simeon</i> (Stefan Nemanja, his father), the first Serbian hagiography.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He has been given various honorific titles, such as "Father" and "Enlightener".<sup id="ref_b_1" class="reference"><a href="#cnote_b">[b]</a></sup> 
</p><p>The Serb people built the cult of St. Sava based on the religious cult; many songs, tales and legends were created about his life, work, merit, goodness, fairness and wisdom, while his relics became a topic of national and ethnopolitical cult and focus of liberation ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In 1840, at the suggestion of <a href="/wiki/Atanasije_Nikoli%C4%87" title="Atanasije Nikolić">Atanasije Nikolić</a>, the rector of the <a href="/wiki/Lyceum_of_the_Principality_of_Serbia" title="Lyceum of the Principality of Serbia">Lyceum</a>, the feast of Saint Sava was chosen to celebrate <a href="/wiki/Education" title="Education">Education</a> every year. It was celebrated as a school holiday until 1945 when the communist authorities abolished it. In 1990, it was reintroduced as a school holiday.<sup id="cite_ref-RTS_2020_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RTS_2020-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Serbian Orthodox Church venerates saint Sava on January 27&#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> January 14&#93;.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biographies">Biographies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Biographies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The first, shorter, biography on St. Sava was written by his successor, Archbishop Arsenije.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The transcript is preserved in a manuscript on <a href="/wiki/Parchment" title="Parchment">parchment</a> dating to the 13th or 14th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>  <a href="/wiki/Domentijan" title="Domentijan">Domentijan</a> (ca. 1210–after 1264), an Athonite monk, wrote the <i>Life of St. Sava</i> in 1253.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiljković2008304–338_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiljković2008304–338-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> He gifted it to Serbian king <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Uro%C5%A1_I" title="Stefan Uroš I">Stefan Uroš I</a> (r. 1243–76).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> This biography describes Sava's life from his birth to his burial in Tarnovo.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>  <a href="/wiki/Teodosije_the_Hilandarian" title="Teodosije the Hilandarian">Teodosije</a> (1246–1328), also an Athonite monk, wrote the <i>Life of St. Sava</i> at Hilandar at the end of the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> He based it on Domentijan's biography, though, unlike the latter, of which narratives are of thoughtful and solemn rhetoric, Teodosije's biography is warmer, with features of a <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographic narrative</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Teodosije's description of events give the impression of a <a href="/wiki/Novel" title="Novel">novel</a>, though it does not distort the historical course of events.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Catholic bishop <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Mrnavi%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Ivan Mrnavić">Ivan Mrnavić</a>, a contemporary of Serbian patriarch <a href="/wiki/Pajsije_of_Janjevo" title="Pajsije of Janjevo">Pajsije</a>, published a biography of St. Sava in Latin, in Rome in 1630–31, which was later translated into Serbian by <a href="/wiki/Veselin_%C4%8Cajkanovi%C4%87" title="Veselin Čajkanović">Veselin Čajkanović</a> (1881–1946); this biography has many historical inaccuracies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> There are many transcripts preserved of Domentijan's biography, and many more of Teodosije's.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>  <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Bosnia" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Bosnia">Bishop of Bosnia</a> <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Thomas_Marnavich" title="Giovanni Thomas Marnavich">Giovanni Thomas Marnavich</a> wrote about him.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
The presence of the relics of St. Sava in Serbia had a church-religious and political significance, especially during the Ottoman period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> No individual among the Serbs has been woven into the consciousness and being of the people as Saint Sava, from his time until the present day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In 1377, <a href="/wiki/Banate_of_Bosnia" title="Banate of Bosnia">Bosnian Ban</a> <a href="/wiki/Tvrtko_I_of_Bosnia" title="Tvrtko I of Bosnia">Tvrtko</a> was crowned King in the presence of Sava's relics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In 1448, <i>vojvoda</i> <a href="/wiki/Stjepan_Vuk%C4%8Di%C4%87_Kosa%C4%8Da" title="Stjepan Vukčić Kosača">Stefan Vukčić Kosača</a> of <a href="/wiki/Zachlumia" title="Zachlumia">Hum</a> styled himself "herzog (duke) of Saint Sava".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The cult collected all South Slavic peoples, especially the Orthodox Serbs, while his grave was also a pilgrim site for Catholics and Muslims.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044_50-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Foreign 16th-century writers, Jean Sesno (1547) and Catherine Zen (1550) noted that Muslims respected the tomb of St. Sava, and feared him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044–45_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200044–45-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Benedicto Ramberti (1553) said that Turks and Jews gave more charity to Mileševa than Serbs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> </p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87,_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save,_1912.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg/220px-Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="129" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg/330px-Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg/440px-Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87%2C_Spaljivanje_Mo%C5%A1tiju_Svetog_Save%2C_1912.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="374" /></a><figcaption>The burning of Saint Sava's relics by the Ottomans after the <a href="/wiki/Banat_Uprising" class="mw-redirect" title="Banat Uprising">Banat Uprising</a>, on April 27, 1595. Painting by <a href="/wiki/Stevan_Aleksi%C4%87" title="Stevan Aleksić">Stevan Aleksić</a> (1912)</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Burning_of_relics">Burning of relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Burning of relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Burning_of_Saint_Sava%27s_relics" title="Burning of Saint Sava&#39;s relics">Burning of Saint Sava's relics</a></div><p>When the <a href="/wiki/Uprising_in_Banat" title="Uprising in Banat">Serbs in Banat rose up</a> against the Ottomans in 1594, using the portrait of Saint Sava on their <a href="/wiki/War_flags" class="mw-redirect" title="War flags">war flags</a>, the Ottomans retaliated by incinerating the relics of St. Sava on the <a href="/wiki/Vra%C4%8Dar_plateau" title="Vračar plateau">Vračar plateau</a> in <a href="/wiki/Belgrade" title="Belgrade">Belgrade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Grand Vizier <a href="/wiki/Koca_Sinan_Pasha" title="Koca Sinan Pasha">Koca Sinan Pasha</a>, the main commander of the Ottoman army, ordered for the relics to be brought from Mileševa to Belgrade, where he set them on fire on 27 April.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEĆirković2004141_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEĆirković2004141-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Monk Nićifor of the <a href="/wiki/Fenek_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Fenek monastery">Fenek monastery</a> wrote that "there was great violence carried out against the clergy and devastation of monasteries".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045_56-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200045-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The Ottomans sought to symbolically and really, set fire to the Serb determination of freedom, which had become growingly noticeable. The event, however, sparked an increase in rebel activity, until the suppression of the uprising in 1595.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESotirović2011164–165_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESotirović2011164–165-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> It is believed that his left hand was saved]; it is currently held at Mileševa.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Sava" title="Church of Saint Sava">Church of Saint Sava</a> was built near the place where his relics were burned. Its construction began in the 1930s and was completed in 2004. It is one of the largest churches in the world.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Divine_Services">Divine Services</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Divine Services">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Divine_Service_(Eastern_Orthodoxy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Service (Eastern Orthodoxy)">Divine Services</a>, <i>službe</i>, were created in his honour following his burial. The earliest service date to the reign of king Vladislav, in which Saint Sava is mentioned along the killed monks on Sinai.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In it, he is compared to the saints <a href="/wiki/Sergius_and_Bacchus" title="Sergius and Bacchus">Sergius and Bacchus</a>, whose relics are held at the Mileševa monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In the service, he is called an <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/illuminator" class="extiw" title="wikt:illuminator">illuminator</a> on earth, and the adoration of his icon is mentioned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> There are two services dedicated to Saint Sava: one dedicated to his <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/assumption" class="extiw" title="wikt:assumption">Assumption</a> (death), and the second to the translation of his relics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Nikola and Radoslav wrote the service on the translation of his relics in ca. 1330.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Other services dedicated to the translation were also compiled in 1599 by <i>inok</i> Georgije, and written by <i>protohegumen</i> Visarion of <a href="/wiki/Zavala_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Zavala monastery">Zavala</a> in 1659–60.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> These services were superseded by the use of Teodosije's service.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The unknown author of the <i>Service of the Assumption of Saint Sava</i>, a monk of Mileševa, speaks to him: "Father of Fathers – [of] clergy rules, wholewised model, virtue of monks, fortification of the church, lighthouse of love, seat of feelings, source of mercifulness, fire-inspired tongue, mouth of sweet words, a church vessel of God, intellectual heaven become – God-good hierarch of Christ".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style></p><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:520px;max-width:520px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:126px;max-width:126px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a
