largest church buildings in the world">the largest churches in the world</a>.</div></div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Churches_dedicated_to_St._Sava">Churches dedicated to St. Sava</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Churches dedicated to St. Sava">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>There are many temples (<i>hramovi</i>) dedicated to St. Sava. As early as the beginning of the 14th century, Serbian Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Nikodim_I" title="Nikodim I">Nikodim I</a> (s. 1316–1324) dedicated a church to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Helena_of_Bulgaria" class="mw-redirect" title="Helena of Bulgaria">Helena of Bulgaria</a>, the wife of Emperor <a href="/wiki/Stefan_Du%C5%A1an" title="Stefan Dušan">Stefan Dušan</a> (r. 1331–55), founded a chapel on the top of the tower in Karyes, dedicated to St. Simeon and St. Sava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> One of the churches of <a href="/wiki/St._Panteleimon_Monastery" title="St. Panteleimon Monastery">Rossikon</a> on Mount Athos, as well as a church in Thessaloniki, are dedicated to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046_58-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Churches throughout Serbia, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Croatia and Montenegro are dedicated to him, as well as churches in <a href="/wiki/Serb_diaspora" title="Serb diaspora">diaspora communities</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046–47_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200046–47-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Visual_arts">Visual arts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Visual arts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Sava,_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_(2).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Saint_Sava%2C_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_%282%29.jpg/220px-Saint_Sava%2C_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_%282%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="276" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Saint_Sava%2C_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_%282%29.jpg/330px-Saint_Sava%2C_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_%282%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f0/Saint_Sava%2C_Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_%282%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="391" data-file-height="491" /></a><figcaption>Fresco in <a href="/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_(monastery)" title="Patriarchate of Peć (monastery)">Patriarchate of Peć</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>There are close to no Serbian churches that do not have a depiction of St. Sava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> He is most often depicted as an <a href="/wiki/Archiereus" class="mw-redirect" title="Archiereus">archiereus</a> (<i>arhijerej</i>, main priest), or together with his father, St. Simeon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The most notable of his fresco depictions are located in the monasteries of <a href="/wiki/Studenica_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Studenica monastery">Studenica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva" class="mw-redirect" title="Mileševa">Mileševa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patriarchate_of_Pe%C4%87_(monastery)" title="Patriarchate of Peć (monastery)">Peć</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mora%C4%8Da_(monastery)" title="Morača (monastery)">Morača</a>, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St._Achillius,_Arilje" title="Church of St. Achillius, Arilje">Arilje</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sopo%C4%87ani" title="Sopoćani">Sopoćani</a>, <a href="/wiki/Visoki_De%C4%8Dani" title="Visoki Dečani">Dečani</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bogorodica_Ljevi%C5%A1ka" class="mw-redirect" title="Bogorodica Ljeviška">Bogorodica Ljeviška</a>, <a href="/wiki/Psa%C4%8Da" class="mw-redirect" title="Psača">Psača</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lesnovo_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Lesnovo monastery">Lesnovo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marko%27s_Monastery" title="Marko&#39;s Monastery">Marko's Monastery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mateji%C4%87_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Matejić Monastery">Matejić</a>, <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St._George,_Staro_Nagori%C4%8Dane" title="Church of St. George, Staro Nagoričane">Nagoričano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Nikita Monastery">Nikita</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrija%C5%A1_Monastery&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Andrijaš Monastery (page does not exist)">Andrijaš</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Bela_Crkva_Karanska&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Bela Crkva Karanska (page does not exist)">Bela Crkva</a>, <a href="/wiki/Baljevac_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Baljevac Church">Baljevac</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pavlica_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Pavlica Monastery">Pavlica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ljubostinja" title="Ljubostinja">Ljubostinja</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manasija" title="Manasija">Resava</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koporin" title="Koporin">Koporin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prohor_P%C4%8Dinjski_Monastery" title="Prohor Pčinjski Monastery">Prohor Pčinjski</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Rudenica&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Rudenica (page does not exist)">Rudenica</a>, Blagoveštenje and St. Nicholas in <a href="/wiki/Ov%C4%8Dar-Kablar_Gorge" title="Ovčar-Kablar Gorge">Ovčar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Je%C5%BEevica" title="Ježevica">Ježevica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poganovo_Monastery" title="Poganovo Monastery">Poganovo</a> and others; he is depicted with the Nemanjić dynasty (<i><a href="/wiki/Nemanji%C4%87_family_tree_(art)" title="Nemanjić family tree (art)">loza Nemanjića</a></i>) in <a href="/wiki/Visoki_De%C4%8Dani" title="Visoki Dečani">Dečani</a>, Peć and <a href="/wiki/Orahovica_Monastery" title="Orahovica Monastery">Orahovica</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The translation of his relics are illustrated in the church of the <a href="/wiki/Gradac_Monastery" title="Gradac Monastery">Gradac Monastery</a>, and in the <a href="/wiki/Patriarchal_Monastery_of_Pe%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarchal Monastery of Peć">Monastery of Peć</a> (in the Bogorodica Odigitrije temple) the scene where Sava appoints his successor Arsenije is depicted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In the Church of St. George, also in the <a href="/wiki/Patriarchal_Monastery_of_Pe%C4%87" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarchal Monastery of Peć">Monastery of Peć</a>, an assembly of Sava is depicted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Iconographer (<i>zograf</i>) <a href="/wiki/Georgije_Mitrofanovi%C4%87" title="Georgije Mitrofanović">Georgije Mitrofanović</a> illustrated events from the <i>Life of St. Sava</i> in the dining room of <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> "The Serbian miracle-workers" Sava and Simeon are depicted in the Archangel Sobor in <a href="/wiki/Kremlin" title="Kremlin">Kremlin</a>, in Moscow.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In the chapel of the <a href="/wiki/Rila_Monastery" title="Rila Monastery">Rila Monastery</a> in Bulgaria, the <i>Life of St. Sava</i> is depicted in eight compositions, and in the Athonite monastery of <a href="/wiki/St._Panteleimon_Monastery" title="St. Panteleimon Monastery">St. Panteleimon Monastery</a> he is depicted as a monk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Sava is depicted with St. Simeon on an icon from the 14th century which is held in the National Museum in Belgrade, and on an icon held in the National Museum in Bucharest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The pair is depicted on tens of icons held in Hilandar.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Other icons of them are found in the monasteries of <a href="/wiki/Lepavina_Monastery" title="Lepavina Monastery">Lepavina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Krka_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Krka Monastery">Krka</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047_64-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200047-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> and on the <a href="/wiki/Triptych" title="Triptych">triptych</a> of Orahovica.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> On an icon of Morača, beside a scene from his life, he is depicted with St. Simeon, <i>knez</i> Stefan and St. Cyril the Philosopher.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Graphical illustrations of St. Sava are found in old Serbian printed books: <i>Triode</i> from the <a href="/wiki/Mrk%C5%A1ina_crkva_printing_house" title="Mrkšina crkva printing house">Mrkšina crkva printing house</a> (1566), <i>Zbornik</i> of <a href="/wiki/Jakov_of_Kamena_Reka" title="Jakov of Kamena Reka">Jakov of Kamena Reka</a> (1566), as well as <i>Sabornik</i> of <a href="/wiki/Bo%C5%BEidar_Vukovi%C4%87" title="Božidar Vuković">Božidar Vuković</a> (1546) where he is depicted with St. Simeon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> There are notable depictions of Sava in <a href="/wiki/Chalcography" class="mw-redirect" title="Chalcography">chalcography</a>, one of which was made by <a href="/wiki/Zaharije_Orfelin" title="Zaharije Orfelin">Zaharije Orfelin</a> (1726–1785).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In Hilandar, there are two wood-cuts depicting St. Sava and St. Simeon holding the <i><a href="/wiki/Three-handed_Theotokos" class="mw-redirect" title="Three-handed Theotokos">Three-handed Theotokos</a></i> icon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> His person is illustrated on numerous liturgical metal and textile items, while he and scenes from his life are illuminated in many manuscripts and printed books.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048_54-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200048-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Many Serbian poets have written poetry dedicated to St. Sava. These include <a href="/wiki/Jovan_Jovanovi%C4%87_Zmaj" title="Jovan Jovanović Zmaj">Jovan Jovanović Zmaj</a>'s (1833–1904) <i>Pod ikonom Svetog Save</i> and <i>Suze Svetog Save</i>, <a href="/wiki/Vojislav_Ili%C4%87" title="Vojislav Ilić">Vojislav Ilić</a>'s (1860–1894) <i>Sveti Sava</i> and <i>Srpkinjica</i>, <a href="/wiki/Milorad_Popovi%C4%87_%C5%A0ap%C4%8Danin" title="Milorad Popović Šapčanin">Milorad Popović Šapčanin</a>'s (1841–1895) <i>Svetom Savi</i>, <a href="/wiki/Aleksa_%C5%A0anti%C4%87" title="Aleksa Šantić">Aleksa Šantić</a>'s (1868–1924) <i>Pred ikonom Svetog Save</i>, <i>Pepeo Svetog Save</i>, <i>Sveti Sava na golgoti</i>, <a href="/wiki/Vojislav_Ili%C4%87_Mla%C4%91i" title="Vojislav Ilić Mlađi">Vojislav Ilić Mlađi</a>'s (1877–1944) <i>Sveti Sava</i>, <a href="/wiki/Nikolaj_Velimirovi%C4%87" title="Nikolaj Velimirović">Nikolaj Velimirović</a>'s (1881–1956) <i>Svetitelju Savo</i>, <i>Reči Svetog Save</i> and <i>Pesma Svetom Savi</i>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Milan_Petrovi%C4%87_(poet)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Milan Petrović (poet) (page does not exist)">Milan Petrović</a>'s (1902–1963) <i>Sveti Sava</i>, <a href="/wiki/Vasko_Popa" title="Vasko Popa">Vasko Popa</a>'s (1922–1991) <i>St. Sava's Journey</i>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Mom%C4%8Dilo_Te%C5%A1i%C4%87&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Momčilo Tešić (page does not exist)">Momčilo Tešić</a>'s (1911–1992) <i>Svetom Savi</i>, <a href="/wiki/Desanka_Maksimovi%C4%87" title="Desanka Maksimović">Desanka Maksimović</a>'s (1898–1993) <i>Savin monolog</i>, <a href="/wiki/Matija_Be%C4%87kovi%C4%87" title="Matija Bećković">Matija Bećković</a>'s (b. 1939) <i>Priča o Svetom Savi</i>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Mi%C4%87o_Jeli%C4%87_Grnovi%C4%87&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Mićo Jelić Grnović (page does not exist)">Mićo Jelić Grnović</a>'s (b. 1942) <i>Uspavanka</i>, and others.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The earliest works of Sava were dedicated to ascetic and monastic life: the Karyes Typikon and Hilandar Typikon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In their nature, they are Church law, based strictly on non-literary works, however, in them some moments came to expression of indirect importance for the establishment of an atmosphere in which Sava's original and in the narrow sense, literary works, came to exist.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In addition, characteristics of Sava's language and style come to light here, especially in those paragraphs which are his specific interpretations or independent supplements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._12-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg/270px-Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg" decoding="async" width="270" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg/405px-Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg/540px-Karyes_Typicon_and_Sava%27s_signature.jpg 2x" data-file-width="712" data-file-height="352" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Karyes_Typikon" title="Karyes Typikon">Karyes Typikon</a> with Sava's signature (1199).</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Karyes_Typikon" title="Karyes Typikon">Karyes Typikon</a>, written for the Karyes cell in 1199. It is basically a translation from a standard Greek ascetic typikon. It became a model for Serbian solitary or eremitical monasticism also outside of Mount Athos.<sup id="cite_ref-Bogdanovic-Karejski_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bogdanovic-Karejski-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Hilandar_Typikon" class="mw-redirect" title="Hilandar Typikon">Hilandar Typikon</a>, written for Hilandar in 1199. Compiled as a translation and adaptation of the introductory part of the Greek Theotokos Euergetis typikon from Constantinople. Sava only used some parts of that typikon, adding his own, different regulations tailored to the needs of Hilandar. He and his father had donated to the Euergetis monastery, and Sava stayed there on his trips to Constantinople, seemingly, he liked the order and way of life in this monastery. This typikon was to become the general managing order for other Serbian monasteries (with small modifications, Sava wrote the Studenica Typikon in 1208).<sup id="cite_ref-Bogdanovic-Hilandarski1_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bogdanovic-Hilandarski1-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The Hilandar Typikon contains regulations for the spiritual life in the monastery and organization of various services of the monastic community (<i>opštežića</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-Bogdanovic-Hilandarski2_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bogdanovic-Hilandarski2-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>The organization of the Serbian church with united areas was set on a completely new basis. The activity of major monasteries developed; caretaking of missionary work was put under the duty of the proto-priests (<i>protopopovi</i>). Legal regulations of the Serbian Church was constituted with a code of a new, independent, compilation of Sava – the <i>Nomocanon</i> or <i>Krmčija</i>; with this codification of Byzantine law, Serbia already at the beginning of the 13th century received a firm legal order and became a state of law, in which the rich Greek-Roman law heritage was continued.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESedlar1994307_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESedlar1994307-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEŠarkić2011241–248_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEŠarkić2011241–248-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis,_1262.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg/191px-Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg" decoding="async" width="191" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg/286px-Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg/382px-Savino_Zakonopravilo_-_Ilovichki_prepis%2C_1262.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1248" data-file-height="1426" /></a><figcaption>1262 transcript of the <i><a href="/wiki/Zakonopravilo" title="Zakonopravilo">Zakonopravilo</a></i> (1220).</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li>Nomocanon (sr. <i><a href="/wiki/Zakonopravilo" title="Zakonopravilo">Zakonopravilo</a></i>) or <i>Krmčija</i>, most likely created in Thessaloniki in 1220, when Sava returned from Nicaea to Serbia, regarding the organization of the new, autocephalous Serbian church. It was a compilation of state ("civil") law and religious rules or canons, with interpretations of famous Byzantine canonists, who by themselves were a kind of source of law. As Byzantine nomocanons, with or without interpretation, the Serbian Nomocanon was a capital source and monument of law; in the medieval Serbian state, it was the source of the first order as a "<a href="/wiki/Divine_right_of_kings" title="Divine right of kings">divine right</a>"; after it, legislations of Serbian rulers (including <a href="/wiki/Du%C5%A1an%27s_Code" title="Dušan&#39;s Code">Dušan's Code</a>) were created. Sava was the initiator of the creation of this compilation, while the translation was likely the work of various authors, older and contemporary to Sava. An important fact is that the choice of compilation in this nomocanon was unique: it is not preserved in Greek manuscript tradition. In the ecclesiastic term, it is very characteristic, due to its opposing of that period's views in effect on church-state relations in Byzantium, and restoring of some older conceptions with which the sovereignty of divine law is insisted on.<sup id="cite_ref-Bogdanovic-Krmcija_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bogdanovic-Krmcija-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>His liturgical regulations include also <i>Psaltir-holding laws</i> (Ustav za držanje Psaltira), which he translated from Greek, or as possibly is the case with the Nomocanon, was only the initiator and organizer, and supervisor of the translation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._18_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._18-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> A personal letter of his, written from Jerusalem to his disciple <i>hegumen</i> Spiridon in Studenica, shows Sava getting closer to literature. This is the first work of the epistolary genre that has been preserved in the old Serbian literature. Theologian Lazar Mirković (1885–1968) noted "With a lot of feeling and longing for the fatherland in a distant world and caring for things in the homeland, Sava wrote this letter to Spiridon, reporting about him and his entourage, of them falling ill on the road, how they donated to the Holy sites, where he intended to travel, and along with the letter he sent gifts: a cross, pleat, cloth and pebbles. The cross and pleat had laid on Christ's grave, and hence these gifts received greater value. Sava perhaps found the cloth in Jordan".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._19_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._19-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The letter has been preserved in 14th-century copies held in the <a href="/wiki/Velika_Remeta_monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Velika Remeta monastery">Velika Remeta monastery</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> The proper literary nature of Sava is however revealed only in his hagiographical and poetic compositions. Each in its genre, they stand at the beginning of the development of convenient literary genres in the independent Serbian literature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._20_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._20-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Hilandar Typikon, Sava included the <i>Short Hagiography of St. Simeon Nemanja</i>, which tells of Simeon's life between his arrival at Hilandar and death. It was written immediately after his death, in 1199 or 1200. The developed hagiography on St. Simeon was written in the introduction of the Studenica Typikon (1208).<sup id="cite_ref-Kratko_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kratko-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Life_of_St._Simeon" class="mw-redirect" title="Life of St. Simeon">Hagiography of St. Simeon</a>, written in 1208 as a <i>ktetor</i> hagiography of the founder of Studenica.<sup id="cite_ref-Kratko_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kratko-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> It was made according to the rules of Byzantine literature.<sup id="cite_ref-Kratko_76-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kratko-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The hagiography itself, biography of a saint, was one of the main prose genres in Byzantium.<sup id="cite_ref-Kratko_76-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kratko-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Hagiographies were written to create or spread the cult of the saint, and communicated the qualities of and virtues of the person in question.<sup id="cite_ref-Kratko_76-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kratko-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The work focused on the monastic character of Simeon, using biographical information as a subset to his renouncing of the throne, power and size in the world for the Kingdom of Heaven.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Simeon is portrayed as a dramatic example of renouncing earthly life, as a representative of basic evangelical teachings and foundations of these, especially of monastic spirituality.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> His biographical pre-history (conquests and achievements) with praises are merged in the prelude, followed by his monastic feats and his death, ending with a prayer instead of praise.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24_77-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._24-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The language is direct and simple, without excessive rhetorics, in which a close witness and companion, participant in the life of St. Simeon, is recognized (in Sava).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._25_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._25-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Milan Kašanin noted that "no old biography of ours is that little pompous and that little rhetorical, and that warm and humane as Nemanja's biography".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._25_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._25-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Very few manuscripts of the works of St. Sava have survived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Apart from the Karyes Typikon, of which copy, a scroll, is today held at Hilandar, it is believed that there are no original manuscript (<i>authograph</i>) of St. Sava.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The original of the Charter of Hilandar (1198) was lost in World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи_79-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Рукописи-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Sava is regarded the founder of the independent <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Serbian_literature" title="Medieval Serbian literature">medieval Serbian literature</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._3–5_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBogdanović1999Предговор,_para._3–5-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ktetor"><i>Ktetor</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Ktetor">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sveti_Sava,_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice,_XIV_vek.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg/220px-Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="627" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg/330px-Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg/440px-Sveti_Sava%2C_freska_iz_Gra%C4%8Danice%2C_XIV_vek.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1400" data-file-height="3992" /></a><figcaption>Fresco in <a href="/wiki/Gra%C4%8Danica_Monastery" title="Gračanica Monastery">Gracanica Monastery</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Sava founded and reconstructed churches and monasteries wherever he stayed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> While staying at Vatopedi, even before the arrival of his father (1197), he founded three chapels (<i>paraklisi</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He had the monastery church covered in <a href="/wiki/Lead" title="Lead">lead</a>, and was regarded the second <i><a href="/wiki/Ktetor" title="Ktetor">ktetor</a></i>, also having donated highly valuable ecclesiastical art objects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041_37-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200041-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Together with his father he was the great, second <i>ktetor</i> of the monasteries of <a href="/wiki/Iviron" class="mw-redirect" title="Iviron">Iviron</a>, <a href="/wiki/Great_Lavra" title="Great Lavra">Great Lavra</a> and churches in <a href="/wiki/Karyes,_Mount_Athos" title="Karyes, Mount Athos">Karyes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The most important was <a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a>, together with his father (1198).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He then founded the cell at Karyes, and in 1199 became ktetor of three more Authonite monasteries: <a href="/wiki/Karakallou" class="mw-redirect" title="Karakallou">Karakallou</a>, <a href="/wiki/Xeropotamou" class="mw-redirect" title="Xeropotamou">Xeropotamou</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Philotheou" class="mw-redirect" title="Philotheou">Philotheou</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In 1197 he gave a large contribution to the Constantinopolitan monastery of the Holy Mother of God Euergetes, and did the same to Philokallou in Thessaloniki; "due to him also giving much gold for the erection of that monastery, the population there regard him the <i>ktetor</i>", according to Teodosije.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Returning to Serbia in 1206, Sava continued his work. The Mother of God Church in Studenica was painted, and two hermitages near Studenica were endowed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> His most important architectural work was the Home of the Holy Saviour, called Žiča, the first seat of the Serbian Archbishopric.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In Peć he built the Church of the Holy Apostles, and he was also involved in the building of the <a href="/wiki/Mile%C5%A1eva" class="mw-redirect" title="Mileševa">Mileševa</a> monastery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a>, on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Sinai" title="Mount Sinai">Mount Sinai</a>, he founded the Monastery of St. John the Apostle, as a shelter for Serb pilgrims.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Sava donated gold to many monasteries in Palestine, Thessaloniki, and especially Mount Athos.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> His ktetor activity was an expression of deep devotion and sincere loyalty to Christian ideals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042_44-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200042-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Hilandar" title="Hilandar">Hilandar</a> monastery on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Karyes" title="Karyes">Karyes</a> monastery cell (see: <a href="/wiki/Karyes_Typicon" class="mw-redirect" title="Karyes Typicon">Karyes Typicon</a>)</li>
<li>Church of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a> in <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a></li></ul>
<p>And many other churches across Serbia, as well.
</p>
<table>
<tbody><tr>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Annotations">Annotations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Sava&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Annotations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div style=""><ol class="references" style="list-style-type:upper-alpha;">
<li id="cnote_a"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#ref_a_1">^</a></b></span> <div style="display:inline;" class="reference-text">Sources puts the year of his birth in either 1169 or 1174.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200037_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200037-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.spc.rs/sr/sveti_sava_prvi_arhiepiskop_prosvetitelj_srpski">official site of the Serbian Orthodox Church</a> put it "around year 1174". Historians Slobodan Mileusnić<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200037_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMileusnić200037-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Alexis_Vlasto" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexis Vlasto">Alexis Vlasto</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218_10-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVlasto1970218-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> supports ca. 1174. </div></li>
<li id="cnote_b"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#ref_b_1">^</a></b></span> <div style="display:inline;" class="reference-text">Throughout history, Sava has been given various honorific titles. He received the popular epithet "Illuminator (enlightener) of the Serbs".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1994118_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1994118-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> In other genealogies and chronicles, as well as in many records and inscriptions, he was given the titles: "First Archbishop and teacher and educator and with God enlighter of his fatherland", "First saint and teacher", "Great miracle-worker", etc. (for further information: <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output
